The theory and applications of anticolimits
Calin Tataru, Jamie Vicary

Abstract
Colimits are a fundamental construction in category theory. They provide a way to construct new objects by gluing together existing objects that are related in some way. We introduce a complementary notion of anticolimits , which provide a way to decompose an object into a colimit of other objects. While anticolimits are not unique in general, we establish that in the presence of pullbacks, there is a “canonical” anticolimit which characterises the existence of other anticolimits. We also provide convenient techniques for computing anticolimits, by changing either the shape or ambient category. The main motivation for this work is the development of a new method, known as anticontraction , for constructing homotopies in the proof assistant homotopy.io for finitely presented n n -categories. Anticontraction complements the existing contraction method and facilitates the construction of homotopies increasing the complexity of a term, enhancing the usability of the proof assistant. For example, it simplifies the naturality move and third Reidemeister move.



1
Introduction

Colimits are an important tool in computer science for gluing things together.
A notable example is
double pushout graph rewriting

[
7
,
6
]
which uses pushouts to glue the right-hand side of a rewrite in the place of a matching subgraph.
Colimits have also been applied to merge patches in a version control system
[
1
]
, compose open Petri nets
[
2
]
, compose systems in reinforcement learning
[
3
]
, give a formal semantics of visual programming
[
8
]
, model multiple inheritance in programming languages
[
11
]
, and compose specifications in formal software development
[
15
]
.

In each of these examples, one can ask the following question: when does a particular object arise as a colimit of some given shape?
To answer this, we introduce the notion of
anticolimits
.
This construction allow us to take an object, equipped with a family of incoming morphisms, and express it as the colimit of a poset-shaped diagram.
Anticolimits may not exist in general, and unlike colimits, they are not guaranteed to be unique.
We may also consider
anticocones
, a similar construction where we do not require the universal property.

To illustrate this, consider the case of pushouts.
Recall that, for a pair of morphisms
p:D\to A
and
q:D\to B
, their pushout is a pair of morphisms
f:A\to C
and
g:B\to C
such that the resulting square commutes and satisfies a universal property:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Here, the solid arrows are the input to the pushout and the dashed arrows represent the output.
The universal property implies that pushouts are unique up to isomorphism.

The corresponding notion of anticolimit is called an
antipushout
, and is defined as follows.
Given a pair of morphisms
f:A\to C
and
g:B\to C
, an antipushout is given by morphisms
p:D\to A
and
q:D\to B
such that the following square is a pushout:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

If a pushout is understood as gluing two objects together along a common part to form a new object, then an antipushout gives us a way to decompose an object into such a gluing arrangement.

General results

In
Section

2
we study the abstract theory of anticolimits.
We show that in the presence of pullbacks, a canonical anticocone can be found for any posetal diagram shape, which is terminal in a category of anticocones; furthermore, if any anticolimit exists, then the canonical anticocone will also be an anticolimit.
Unpacking this for antipushouts, we obtain the following corollary:
if a pair of morphisms have a pullback, then they have an antipushout iff the pullback is an antipushout:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{C}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}

We also provide general techniques for constructing anticolimits from existing anticolimits, by changing either the shape of the diagrams, or the category in which they live.
We use these techniques to give an explicit construction procedure for anticolimits of sets, and then extend this to construct anticolimits of posets, preorders, and finite ordinals.

Application

The motivating application for this work lies in the proof assistant
homotopy.io
for
n
-dimensional string diagrams, which is based on the theory of associative
n
-categories.
The main mechanism for proof construction is
contraction
, presented at LICS 2019
[
13
]
, which uses a colimit operation to perform a homotopy contraction of part of the string diagram.
This is convenient when simplifying proof objects, but is far less elegant when we need to create nontrivial structure.

We illustrate this problem in
Figure

1
, showing the traditional proof assistant interaction steps required to construct the 4-cell representing the naturality move for the braiding.
A total of five user interaction steps are required, most of which are non-obvious.
With anticontraction, the procedure is far more straightforward; the new workflow is given in
Figure

2
, which shows that only two moves are now necessary, both of which are intuitively obvious.

For a more intricate example, we consider the 4-cell representing the Reidemeister III move from knot theory; in terms of the terminology of a braided monoidal category, this can be considered “naturality for the braiding”.
Using anticontraction, we can simplify this workflow from fifteen steps (see
Figure

3
) down to seven steps (see
Figure

4
).

We develop the necessary theory for this in
Sections

3
and
4
, including the relevant background on the theory of zigzag categories, which gives the inductive basis for the string diagram formalism.
We develop a series of results which show factorisation structures can be lifted through the zigzag construction, enabling anticontractions to the computed recursively.

Figure 4:
Reidemeister III with anticontraction.

The authors have implemented anticontraction, and the feature is now available within a pre-release version of the proof assistant hosted at

https://beta.homotopy.io

To use anticontraction, generators need to be marked as ‘oriented’ in the signature, via the settings dialog for each generator; this disables certain geometrical features not currently compatible with anticontraction. To generate the moves of
Figure

2
, create the first image; then drag the vertex
up
to create the second image; then drag the vertex
right
to create the third image.

1.1
Related work

We are not aware of any work on anticolimits in the literature.
The closest relevant notion may be
pushout complements
which play an important role in double pushout graph rewriting.
These resemble our notion of antipushouts, in that they both take as input one side of a pushout square and produce the other side:

•

pushout complements take a composable pair of morphisms and produce the other composable pair;

•

antipushouts take two morphisms with a common codomain and produce two morphisms with a common domain.

We illustrate this as follows, where the solid arrows are given as input to the construction, and the dashed arrows are produced:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{aligned}\!\!\begin{array}[]{l}\text{antipushout}\end{array}

The work on anticontraction builds on the existing body of work on associative
n
-categories and the proof assistant
homotopy.io
.
The theory of associative
n
-categories was originally developed by Dorn, Douglas, and Vicary, and described in Dorn’s PhD thesis
[
5
]
.
We follow the development presented by Reutter and Vicary at LICS 2019
[
13
]
.
Further aspects of the proof assistant have been described by Corbyn, Heidemann, Hu, Sarti, Tataru, and Vicary
[
10
,
9
,
14
,
17
]
.

1.2
Notation

We adopt the following notation throughout the paper:

•

\mathbf{n}
is the totally ordered set
\{0,\dots,n-1\}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

\mathbf{\Delta}
is the category of finite ordinals and monotone maps.

•

\boldsymbol{\nabla}\subset\mathbf{\Delta}
is the category of finite intervals (i.e. finite non-empty ordinals) and monotone maps that preserve top and bottom.

•

\mathbf{Set}
is the category of sets and functions.

•

\mathbf{Pos}
is the category of posets and monotone maps.

•

\mathbf{Pre}
is the category of preorders and monotone maps.

•

\mathbf{Cat}
is the category of (small) categories and functors.

•

Given a poset
\mathbf{J}
and an object
i\in\mathbf{J}
, define the following:

\upper i\coloneqq\{j\in\mathbf{J}\mid i\leq j\}\qquad\Upper i\coloneqq\max(\upper i)

•

[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}]
is the functor category between categories
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{D}
.

1.3
Acknowledgements

We are grateful to Thibaut Benjamin, Lukas Heidemann, Nick Hu, Ioannis Markakis, Wilf Offord, and Chiara Sarti for useful discussions.

2
Anticolimits

Let
\mathbf{J}
be a poset, seen as a thin category.
Given a diagram
F:\mathbf{J}\to\mathbf{C}
, a cocone over
F
can be equivalently defined as either one of:

1.

a natural transformation
\kappa:F\Rightarrow\Delta_{c}
, or

2.

a sink
\kappa:F|_{\max\mathbf{J}}\Rightarrow\Delta_{c}
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{k}}

commutes for every
i\in\mathbf{J}
and
j,k\in\Upper i
.

While (1) is the standard definition, we will use (2) as our definition of cocone, because it requires the least amount of data.
A colimit is a cocone which is universal, i.e. any cocone factors uniquely through it.
Now the problem we are interested in is the following: given a sink
\kappa
over
\max\mathbf{J}
, find all diagrams over
\mathbf{J}
for which
\kappa
is a colimit.

Definition 1
.

Let
\mathbf{J}
be a poset and consider a sink

\kappa:X\Rightarrow\Delta_{c}:\max\mathbf{J}\to\mathbf{C}

We define a
\mathbf{J}
-anticocone
of
\kappa
to be a diagram
A:\mathbf{J}\to\mathbf{C}
which extends
X
such that
\kappa
is a cocone over it.
We say that an anticocone is an
anticolimit
if furthermore it makes
\kappa
into a colimit.

For example, consider the following poset
\mathbf{J}
(i.e. a span):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

This has two maximal elements, so a sink over
\max\mathbf{J}
is a cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

The
\mathbf{J}
-anticocones are spans
(p:D\to A,\,q:D\to B)
such that
f\circ p=g\circ q
,
and the
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits, which we call
antipushouts
, are spans making the following diagram into a pushout square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Example 2
.

Consider the cospan in the category
\mathbf{\Delta}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{3}}

Then the following spans are both antipushouts of the cospan:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0001}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0122}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0111}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0012}

Here, we write
a_{1}\dots a_{n}:\mathbf{n}\to\mathbf{m}
for the map that sends
i
to
a_{i}
.

In general, anticolimits may not exist, and even if they do, they are not necessarily unique.
They do not satisfy a universal property.
There is an easy way to test if a sink cannot have anticolimits.

Proposition 3
.

If
\kappa
has an anticolimit, then it is jointly epic.

Proof.

Colimit inclusions are guaranteed to be jointly epic.
Also,
\{\kappa_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbf{J}}
is jointly epic iff
\{\kappa_{i}\}_{i\in\max\mathbf{J}}
is jointly epic, because each
\kappa_{i}
for
i
non-maximal factorises through some
\kappa_{j}
for
j
maximal.
∎

Anticocones and anticolimits form categories.
First, we need to define the category of extensions of one functor along another.

Definition 4
.

An
extension
of a functor
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
along another functor
G:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{E}
is a functor
H:\mathbf{E}\to\mathbf{D}
such that
H\circ G=F
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}

The category
\Ext_{G}(F)
of extensions of
F
along
G
is the subcategory of
[\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}]
obtained by the following pullback in
\mathbf{Cat}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{C},\mathbf{D}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

In particular, the morphisms are given by natural transformations
\eta:H_{1}\to H_{2}
such that
\eta\circ G=\id_{F}
.
If
i:\mathbf{C}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{E}
is a subcategory inclusion, we write
\Ext_{\mathbf{E}}(F)
instead of
\Ext_{i}(F)
; the morphisms here are natural transformations whose components on
\mathbf{C}
are trivial.

Definition 5
.

The categories
\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
of anticocones and
\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
of anticolimits are defined to be full subcategories of
\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)
:

\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\hookrightarrow\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\hookrightarrow\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)

Sieves and cosieves

Recall that a
sieve
in a category
\mathbf{C}
is a full subcategory of
\mathbf{C}
closed under precomposition with morphisms in
\mathbf{C}
, according to
[
12
, Definition 6.2.2.1]
.
Dually, a
cosieve
is a full subcategory closed under postcomposition with morphisms in
\mathbf{C}
.

We have the following results about the categories of anticocones and anticolimits.
The full proofs are included in the appendix.

Lemma 6
.

\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is a sieve in
\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
\eta:A\to B
be a morphism in
\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)
and suppose that
B
is an anticocone of
\kappa
.
Then the following commutes for every
i\in\mathbf{J}
and
j,k\in\Upper i
since
\kappa
is a cocone over
B
and by naturality of
\eta
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Aj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ai}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i}}

Therefore
\kappa
is a cocone over
A
, which implies that
A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
.
∎

Lemma 7
.

\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is a cosieve in
\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
\eta:A\to B
be a morphism in
\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(X)
and suppose that
A
is an anticolimit of
\kappa
.
Now let
\lambda:X\Rightarrow\Delta_{d}
be a cocone over
B
.
Then the following commutes for every
i\in\mathbf{J}
and
j,k\in\Upper i
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Aj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ai}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i}}

Hence
\lambda
is also a cocone over
A
, so there is universal morphism
u:c\to d
such that
\lambda=\Delta_{u}\circ\kappa
.
Therefore
\kappa
is a colimit of
B
.
∎

Lemma 8
.

If
\eta:A\to B
is a pointwise epimorphism in
\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)
and
B
is an anticolimit of
\kappa
, then
A
is also an anticolimit of
\kappa
.

Proof.

We already have that
\kappa
is a cocone over
A
by
Lemma

6
.
Now let
\lambda:X\Rightarrow\Delta_{d}
be a cocone over
A
.
Then the outer diagram below commutes for every
i\in\mathbf{J}
and
j,k\in\Upper i
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Aj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ai}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i}}

Since
\eta_{i}
is epic, the following diagram also commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{k}}

Hence
\lambda
is also a cocone over
B
, so there is universal morphism
u:c\to d
such that
\lambda=\Delta_{u}\circ\kappa
.
Therefore
\kappa
is a colimit of
A
.
∎

2.1
Pullback construction

For any poset
\mathbf{J}
and sink
\kappa
, we can define a
\mathbf{J}
-anticocone
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
by a pullback construction.
When this exists, we show that it is terminal in
\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
, and that
\kappa
has
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits iff
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is an anticolimit.
Therefore,
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
can be considered the “most general”
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimit of
\kappa
, and can be used to verify the existence of anticolimits.

Definition 9
.

For a poset
\mathbf{J}
and a sink
\kappa
, we define

\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa):\mathbf{J}\to\mathbf{C}

by the following construction:

•

every object
i
is mapped to the pullback of
\{\kappa_{j}\mid j\in\Upper i\}
;

•

every morphism
i\leq j
is mapped to the universal morphism between the respective pullbacks that exists since
\Upper j\subseteq\Upper i
.

If any of these pullbacks fail to exist, then
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
does not exist.

Note that this is an anticocone of
\kappa
by the universal property of pullbacks.
To illustrate how this works, consider a simple example.

Example 10
.

Let
\mathbf{J}=\mathcal{P}^{+}(\mathbf{3})^{\mathrm{op}}
and let
\kappa
be a sink:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}

The associated anticocone
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is given by the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}\times_{C}X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}\times_{C}X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}\times_{C}X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}\times_{C}X_{1}\times_{C}X_{2}}

Lemma 11
.

If
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
exists, it is the terminal object in
\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
.

Proof.

If
A
is a
\mathbf{J}
-anticocone of
\kappa
, then
Ai
is a cone over
\{\kappa_{j}\}_{j\in\Upper i}
for every
i\in\mathbf{J}
, so we have a universal morphism into the pullback
u_{i}:Ai\to\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)(i)
.
Now the following commutes whenever
i\leq j
due to the universal property of pullbacks:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ai}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Aj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)(j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{j}}

As a result, we have a natural transformation

u:A\Rightarrow\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa).

This is trivial on
\max\mathbf{J}
, so it is a morphism in
\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(X)
.
Moreover, it is unique since the components
u_{i}
are unique by construction.
∎

Corollary 12
.

If
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
exists, then the category of anticocones
\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is isomorphic to the slice category
\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)/\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
, with the isomorphism given by the forgetful functor of the slice category.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemmas

6
and
11
.
∎

Theorem 13
.

If
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
exists, either one of the following holds:

•

\kappa
has no
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits, or

•

\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is an anticolimit of
\kappa
.

Proof.

If
\kappa
has an anticolimit
A
, we have a map
A\to\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
in
\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
by
Lemma

11
, so
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is an anticolimit by
Lemma

7
.
∎

Hence, the category
\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is isomorphic to a full subcategory of
\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)/\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
which is empty or contains the terminal object.
Also if
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
happens to have a colimit, we have a universal map:

\colim\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\to c

Then
\kappa
has
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits iff this morphism is an isomorphism.

Corollary 14
.

Given a pair of morphisms
(f,g)
with the same codomain which have a pullback, the following are equivalent:

•

the morphisms
(f,g)
have an antipushout, and

•

the following is simultaneously both a pullback and a pushout:
1
1

1

Such squares are called
bicartesian
or
pulation squares
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times_{C}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\urcorner}

For example, consider the following cospan in
\mathbf{\Delta}
for
n>1
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{n}}

By the previous corollary, it does
not
have any antipushouts, as the pullback is
\mathbf{0}
, and the pushout of the pullback is then
\mathbf{n}
.
Note that this is despite the cospan being jointly epic (as per
Proposition

3
).

2.2
Change of shape

Any monotone map
f:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
induces a functor

(-)\circ f:[\mathbf{J},\mathbf{C}]\to[\mathbf{I},\mathbf{C}]

Under certain conditions, this interacts nicely with anticolimits.

Lemma 15
.

Suppose that we have functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}

1.

\beta
preserves extensions, i.e. if
H
is an extension of
F
along
G_{2}
then
H\circ\beta
is an extension of
F\circ\alpha
along
G_{1}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G_{2}}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G_{1}}(F\circ\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E}_{2},\mathbf{D}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E}_{1},\mathbf{D}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ\beta}

2.

If
\alpha
is epic,
\beta
reflects extensions, i.e. if
H\circ\beta
is an extension of
F\circ\alpha
along
G_{1}
then
H
is an extension of
F
along
G_{2}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G_{2}}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G_{1}}(F\circ\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E}_{2},\mathbf{D}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E}_{1},\mathbf{D}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ\beta}

Proof.

(1) is straightforward.
For (2), we have that

H\circ G_{2}\circ\alpha=H\circ\beta\circ G_{1}=F\circ\alpha\implies H\circ G_{2}=F

∎

Definition 16
.

Let
f:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
be a monotone map that preserves maximal elements.
Then we define the following terminology:

•

f

preserves

\mathbf{J}
-anticocones of
\kappa
if:

\forall A\in\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)\ldotp A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\implies A\circ f\in\Acc_{\mathbf{I}}(\kappa\circ f)

•

f

reflects

\mathbf{J}
-anticocones of
\kappa
if:

\forall A\in\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)\ldotp A\circ f\in\Acc_{\mathbf{I}}(F\circ\kappa)\implies A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)

•

f

preserves

\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits of
\kappa
if:

\forall A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\ldotp A\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\implies A\circ f\in\Acl_{\mathbf{I}}(\kappa\circ f)

•

f

reflects

\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits of
\kappa
if:

\forall A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\ldotp A\circ f\in\Acl_{\mathbf{I}}(F\circ\kappa)\implies A\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)

Definition 17
.

A monotone map
f:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
is
fair
if

•

it preserves and lifts maximal elements:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\max\mathbf{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\max\mathbf{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

•

it lifts pairwise lower bounds of maximal elements:
\mathbf{I}
\mathbf{J}
f
\exists i_{0}
i_{1}
i_{2}
j
fi_{1}
fi_{2}

That is, for all
j\in\mathbf{J}
and
i_{1},i_{2}\in\max\mathbf{J}
with
j\leq fi_{1}
and
j\leq fi_{2}
, there exists
i_{0}\in\mathbf{I}
with
i_{0}\leq i_{1}
and
i_{0}\leq i_{2}
and
j=fi_{0}
.

Final functors

Recall that a functor
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
is
final
if for any functor
G:\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{E}
, there is a natural isomorphism

\colim GF\cong\colim G.

Also recall that
F
is final iff for every
d\in\mathbf{D}
, the comma category
d/F
is connected, i.e. any two objects are connected by a zigzag of morphisms.
Note that if
f:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
is a monotone map, the comma category
j/f
for
j\in\mathbf{J}
is just the sub-poset
f^{-1}(\upper j)\subseteq\mathbf{I}
.

Theorem 18
.

Let
f:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
be a monotone map.

1.

If
f
is fair, it preserves and reflects anticocones:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acc_{\mathbf{I}}(\kappa\circ f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\mathbf{I}}(X\circ f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ f}

2.

If
f
is also final, it preserves and reflects anticolimits:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acl_{\mathbf{I}}(\kappa\circ f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acc_{\mathbf{I}}(\kappa\circ f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\circ f}

Proof.

Note that if
f
is fair, then the class of diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Aj_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Aj_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Aj_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{j_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{j_{2}}}

for
j_{0}\in\mathbf{J}
and
j_{1},j_{2}\in\Upper j_{0}
, is equivalent to the following class of diagrams, for
i_{0}\in\mathbf{I}
and
i_{1},i_{2}\in\Upper i_{0}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Afi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Afi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Afi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{fi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{fi_{2}}}

In particular, the first class is contained in the second class since
f
lifts maximal elements and lower bounds of maximal elements, and vice-versa since
f
preserves maximal elements.
Hence,
f
preserves and reflects anticocones.
Finally, (2) follows immediately from
f
being final.
∎

Therefore, given a fair and final monotone map
f:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
, we can compute
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits in terms of
\mathbf{I}
-anticolimits, which are assumed to be simpler.
In particular, given an
\mathbf{I}
-anticolimit
A
, we can construct an anticolimit in
\mathbf{J}
by extending along
f
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

Note that a particular
\mathbf{I}
-anticolimit may fail to extend.
However, any extension gives a valid
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimit and
all

\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits can be obtained in this way, for any fixed choice of
f:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
.

2.2.1
Removing non-sinking edges

Here we introduce a technique that, for a given poset
\mathbf{J}
, may be used to construct a poset
\mathbf{I}
and a fair and final monotone map
f:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
, such that
\mathbf{I}
is simpler.
In particular,
\mathbf{I}
will have fewer arrows.
Recall that in a poset
\mathbf{J}
, we say that
a
is
covered
by
b
if
a<b
and there is no
c
such that
a<c<b
.

Definition 19
.

Let
\mathbf{J}
be a poset with
a
covered by
b
.
We define

\mathbf{J}\setminus\{a<b\}

to be the poset obtained from
\mathbf{J}
by removing the relation
a<b
.

For example, consider removing the red arrow here:

Lemma 20
.

If
b
is non-maximal, this map is fair and final:

\mathbf{J}\setminus\{a<b\}\to\mathbf{J}

Proof.

Removing
a<b
cannot remove maximal elements and may only create one maximal element, that being
a
if
\upper a=\{a,b\}
.
Since
b
is non-maximal, the maximal elements are the same.

The map does not change the lower bounds of maximal elements, because
a<b
does not participate in any lower bounds of maximal elements, since
b
is non-maximal.
Hence the map is fair. To show that the map is final, we need to show that the subset
\upper i
is connected in
\mathbf{J}\setminus\{a<b\}
for every
i\in\mathbf{J}
.
We have two cases:

1.

If
i\neq a
, then
i
is below every element in
\upper i
.

2.

If
i=a
, then
a
is below every element in
\upper a
except
b
, but
a
and
b
are both below some
c\in\upper a
since
b
is non-maximal.

Therefore,
\upper i
is connected in both cases, so the map is final.
∎

Note that an extension of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{J}\setminus\{i<j\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

consists of finding a morphism
Ai\to Aj
that commutes with the rest of the diagram, which is a tractable problem in many categories.

2.3
Hypergraph-like posets

Consider the class of posets in which every non-maximal element is minimal.
These can be described in terms of hypergraphs.

Definition 21
.

A hypergraph
H=(V,E,i)
consists of:

•

a set
V
of vertices,

•

a set
E
of hyperedges, and

•

an incidence function
i:E\to\mathcal{P}^{+}(V)
.

Proposition 22
.

There is a bijection between:

•

hypergraphs
H=(V,E,i)
, and

•

posets
\mathbf{J}
such that every non-maximal element is minimal.

Proof.

Every hypergraph has an associated poset
V\sqcup E
where
e\leq v
iff
v\in i(e)
.
Conversely, every poset
\mathbf{J}
such that every non-maximal element is minimal is the associated poset of a hypergraph:

V\coloneqq\max\mathbf{J}\qquad E\coloneqq\mathbf{J}\setminus\max\mathbf{J}\qquad i(e)\coloneqq\Upper e

∎

v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}
v_{1}
v_{2}
v_{3}
e_{1}
e_{2}
e_{3}

Figure 5:
Correspondence between hypergraphs and posets.

We say that such a poset is
hypergraph-like
.
Note that a diagram
A
over a hypergraph-like poset can be described as follows:

•

for every vertex
v
, an object
A_{v}
, and

•

for every hyperedge
e
, a source
\{A_{e}\to A_{v}\}_{v\in i(e)}
.

i.e. a collection of objects and generalised relations between them.

As a corollary of
Lemma

20
, we can reduce any poset
\mathbf{J}
to a hypergraph-like poset
\widehat{\mathbf{J}}
such that
\widehat{\mathbf{J}}\to\mathbf{J}
is both fair and final.

2.4
Anticolimits of sets

We will now show how to compute anticolimits in
\mathbf{Set}
.
As shown in the previous section, it suffices to consider hypergraph-like posets, so fix a hypergraph
H=(V,E,i)
and a sink
\{f_{v}:A_{v}\to C\}_{v\in V}
. We construct anticolimits according to the following scheme:

1.

For every
e\in E
, compute the pullback

\Pi_{e}\coloneqq\mathrm{pullback}(\{f_{v}:A_{v}\to C\}_{v\in i(e)})

2.

Choose a family of maps

\{g_{e}:A_{e}\to\Pi_{e}\}_{e\in E}

3.

Compute the colimit of the maps

\{A_{e}\xrightarrow{g_{e}}\Pi_{e}\xrightarrow{\pi_{v}}A_{v}\}_{e\in E,\,v\in i(e)}

Recall that this is given by the following quotient:

Q\coloneqq\coprod_{v\in V}A_{v}/\sim

where
\sim
is the smallest equivalence relation such that

\pi_{v_{1}}(g_{e}(x))\sim\pi_{v_{2}}(g_{e}(x))

whenever
e\in E
, and
v_{1},v_{2}\in i(e)
, and
x\in A_{e}
.

4.

Check that the universal map
u:Q\to C
is an isomorphism.

2.5
Change of category

Any functor
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
induces a functor

F\circ(-):[\mathbf{J},\mathbf{C}]\to[\mathbf{J},\mathbf{D}]

Under certain conditions, this interacts nicely with anticolimits.

Lemma 23
.

Suppose that we have functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}

1.

\alpha
preserves extensions, i.e. if
H
is an extension of
F
along
G
then
\alpha\circ H
is an extension of
\alpha\circ F
along
G
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G}(\alpha\circ F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}_{2}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\circ(-)}

2.

If
\alpha
is monic, it reflects extensions, i.e. if
\alpha\circ H
is an extension of
\alpha\circ F
along
G
then
H
is an extension of
F
along
G
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{G}(\alpha\circ F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbf{E},\mathbf{D}_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\circ(-)}

Proof.

(1) is straightforward.
(2) follows from
\alpha
being monic.
∎

Definition 24
.

Let
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
be a functor.

•

F

preserves

\mathbf{J}
-anticocones of
\kappa
if:

\forall A\in\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)\ldotp A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\implies F\circ A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(F\circ\kappa)

•

F

reflects

\mathbf{J}
-anticocones of
\kappa
if:

\forall A\in\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)\ldotp F\circ A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(F\circ\kappa)\implies A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)

•

F

preserves

\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits of
\kappa
if:

\forall A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\ldotp A\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\implies F\circ A\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(F\circ\kappa)

•

F

reflects

\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits of
\kappa
if:

\forall A\in\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\ldotp F\circ A\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(F\circ\kappa)\implies A\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)

Definition 25
.

A faithful functor
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
is
directly nice
if it preserves and reflects
\mathbf{J}
-colimits, and
indirectly nice
if it preserves
\mathbf{J}
-colimits, and
\mathbf{C}
has pullbacks and
F
preserves them.

Theorem 26
.

Let
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
be a functor.

1.

If
F
is faithful, it preserves and reflects anticocones:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(F\circ\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\mathbf{J}}(F\circ X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ(-)}

2.

If
F
is directly nice, it preserves and reflects anticolimits, and similarly if
F
is indirectly nice and
\kappa
already has anticolimits:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acl_{\mathbf{I}}(F\circ\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acc_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Acc_{\mathbf{I}}(F\circ\kappa)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ(-)}

Proof.

Assume that
F
is faithful, and let
A
be an extension of
X
.
Now for every
i\in\mathbf{J}
and
j,k\in\Upper i
, the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Aj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ai}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{k}}

commutes iff the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fc}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FAj}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FAk}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FAi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\kappa_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\kappa_{k}}

since
F
is faithful.
Hence,
F
preserves and reflects anticocones.

Clearly, if
F
is directly nice, it preserves and reflects anticolimits, and if
F
is indirectly nice, it preserves anticolimits.
Suppose that
\kappa
has anticolimits.
Then by
Theorem

13
,
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
is an anticolimit.
Let
A\to\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
be an anticocone such that
F\circ\kappa
is universal over
F\circ A
.
Now if
\lambda:X\Rightarrow\Delta_{d}
is a cocone over
A
, then
F\circ\lambda
is a cocone over
F\circ A
, so there is a universal map
u^{\prime}:Fc\to Fd
such that

F\circ\lambda=\Delta_{u^{\prime}}\circ F\circ\kappa

Then
F\circ\lambda
is a cocone over
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(F\circ\kappa)
.
Since
F
preserves pullbacks,

\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(F\circ\kappa)=F\circ\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)

Since
F
is faithful,
\lambda
must be a cocone over
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
.
Finally, since
\kappa
is universal over
\Pi_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
, there is a universal map
u:c\to d
with

\lambda=\Delta_{u}\circ\kappa.

Therefore,
\kappa
is universal over
A
, so
F
reflects anticolimits.
∎

Therefore, if a functor
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
is directly or indirectly nice, we can compute anticolimits in the category
\mathbf{C}
in terms of anticolimits in the category
\mathbf{D}
.
In particular, given an anticolimit
A
in
\mathbf{D}
, we can construct an anticolimit in
\mathbf{C}
by finding a lift of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\max\mathbf{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

Note that a particular anticolimit in
\mathbf{D}
may fail to have such a lift.
However, any lift is a valid anticolimit in
\mathbf{C}
and
all
anticolimits in
\mathbf{C}
can be obtained in this way, for any fixed choice of
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
.
If
F
is indirectly nice, this construction only works if, a priori, the sink had anticolimits in
\mathbf{C}
, but that is easy to check using
Theorem

13
.
Finally, if
F
is monic, then it suffices to find a lift of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

i.e. any lift automatically extends
X
, and the lift is unique if it exists.

2.6
Anticolimits of finite ordinals

We will now show how to compute anticolimits in
\mathbf{\Delta}
in terms of anticolimits in
\mathbf{Set}
, using the result from the previous section.

Lemma 27
.

The inclusion
\mathbf{\Delta}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pos}
preserves colimits.

Proof.

Suppose that we have a universal cocone in
\mathbf{\Delta}
:

\eta:F\Rightarrow\Delta_{\mathbf{n}}

We want to show that it is also a universal in
\mathbf{Pos}
, so let
\lambda:F\Rightarrow\Delta_{P}
be a cocone for a poset
P
.
Now
P
has a linear extension
f:P\hookrightarrow L
by the order extension principle
[
16
]
.
Then we have a cocone in
\mathbf{\Delta}
:

\lambda_{L}\coloneqq\Delta_{f}\circ\lambda:F\Rightarrow\Delta_{L}

Hence, there exists a universal map
u_{L}:\mathbf{n}\to L
with
\lambda_{L}=\Delta_{u_{L}}\circ\eta
.
Also by the order extension principle,
P
is the intersection of all its linear extensions
L
, so we can construct a map
u:\mathbf{n}\to P
such that
\lambda=\Delta_{u}\circ\eta
.
Therefore,
\eta
is indeed a universal cocone in
\mathbf{Pos}
.
∎

Corollary 28
.

The inclusion
\mathbf{\Delta}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pos}
is directly nice.

Proof.

The functor is fully faithful, so it reflects colimits.
Also it preserves colimits by the previous lemma.
∎

This means that we can compute anticolimits in
\mathbf{\Delta}
in terms of anticolimits in
\mathbf{Pos}
by lifting each anticolimit in
\mathbf{Pos}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pos}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

This simply consists of checking whether the diagram
A
lies in
\mathbf{\Delta}
.

Lemma 29
.

The forgetful functor
\mathbf{Pre}\to\mathbf{Set}
is indirectly nice.

Proof.

Clearly it is faithful.
Also it preserves limits and colimits, since it has a left adjoint (sending each set to its discrete preorder
2
2
footnotemark:

2

) and a right adjoint (sending each set to its indiscrete preoder
3
3

3

The
discrete preorder
on a set
X
is the preorder
\leq
given by
x\leq x
for all
x\in X
.
).
∎

3
3
footnotetext:
The
indiscrete preorder
on a set
X
is the preorder
\leq
given by
x\leq y
for all
x,y\in X
.

This means that we can compute anticolimits in
\mathbf{Pre}
in terms of anticolimits in
\mathbf{Set}
by lifting each anticolimit in
\mathbf{Set}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\max\mathbf{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Pre}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

This consists of choosing preorders on
Ai
for
i
non-maximal that are compatible with each other and the preorders on
Xi
for
i
maximal.

Finally, consider the inclusion
\mathbf{Pos}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Pre}
.
It does not preserve colimits, however it has a left adjoint which does:
4
4

4

This sends every preorder
P
to the poset
P/\sim
given by
a\sim b
iff
a\leq b
and
b\leq a
.

L:\mathbf{Pre}\to\mathbf{Pos}

Hence, we can compute anticolimits in
\mathbf{Pos}
in terms of anticolimits in
\mathbf{Pre}
via the following procedure, for every sink
\kappa
in
\mathbf{Pos}
:

1.

Choose a lift
\widetilde{\kappa}
of
\kappa
along
L
.

2.

Choose an anticolimit
A
of
\widetilde{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{Pre}
.

3.

Therefore
LA
is an anticolimit of
\kappa
in
\mathbf{Pos}
.

Note that
all
anticolimits in
\mathbf{Pos}
can be obtained in this way, for the following reason.
If
A
is an anticolimit of
\kappa
in
\mathbf{Pos}
, then
A
has a colimit
\widetilde{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{Pre}
, and moreover
L\widetilde{\kappa}=\kappa
since
L
preserves colimits.

3
Zigzag category

The zigzag category construction yields a simple inductive definition of the terms of the theory of associative
n
-categories, known as
n
-diagrams
.
Following Reutter and Vicary
[
13
]
, we review the basic elements of the theory. This section is mostly background, although some of the presentation is novel.

Definition 30
.

In a category
\mathbf{C}
, a
zigzag

X
is a finite sequence of cospans:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg n)}

This has
length

\lvert X\rvert=n\geq 0
.
The objects
X(\sing i)
for
i\in\mathbf{n}
are called the
singular objects
, and
X(\reg j)
for
j\in\mathbf{n+1}
the
regular objects
.

To define a notion of morphisms between zigzags, we first recall the duality between ordinals and intervals due to Wraith
[
18
]
:

\Reg:\mathbf{\Delta}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\boldsymbol{\nabla}^{\mathrm{op}}

This maps each
\mathbf{n}
to
\mathbf{n+1}
and each monotone map
f:\mathbf{n}\to\mathbf{m}
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
to the monotone map
\Reg f:\mathbf{m+1}\to\mathbf{n+1}
in
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
given by

(\Reg f)(i)\coloneqq\min(\{j\in\mathbf{n}\mid f(j)\geq i\}\cup\{n\}).

This has a nice geometrical intuition, which is illustrated in
Figure

6
: the map
f
going up the page is “interleaved” with the map
\Reg f
going down the page.
We can obtain the definition of a zigzag map from this picture, by instantiating the arrows with actual morphisms.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\bullet}}

Figure 6:
An interleaving of
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f:\mathbf{3}\to\mathbf{4}
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
going up, and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Reg f:\mathbf{5}\to\mathbf{4}
in
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
going down.

Definition 31
.

In a category
\mathbf{C}
, a
zigzag map

f:X\to Y
between a zigzag
X
of length
n
and and a zigzag
Y
of length
m
consists of the following:

•

a
singular map
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
,

f_{\mathsf{s}}:\mathbf{n}\to\mathbf{m}

with an implied
regular map
in
\boldsymbol{\nabla}
,

f_{\mathsf{r}}\coloneqq\Reg f_{\mathsf{s}}:\mathbf{m+1}\to\mathbf{n+1}

•

for every
i\in\mathbf{n}
, a
singular slice

f(\sing i):X(\sing i)\to Y(\sing f_{\mathsf{s}}(i))

•

for every
j\in\mathbf{m+1}
, a
regular slice

f(\reg j):X(\reg f_{\mathsf{r}}(j))\to Y(\reg j)

These must satisfy the following conditions for every
i\in\mathbf{m}
:

•

if
f_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1}(i)
is empty, this diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg i+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg f_{\mathsf{r}}(i))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\reg i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\reg i+1)}

•

if
f_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1}(i)=[a,b]
is non-empty, these diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\reg i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\sing a)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg i+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg b+1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\sing b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\reg i+1)}

and the following diagram commutes for every
a\leq j<b
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg j+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing j+1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\sing j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(\sing j+1)}

These unique composites are called
diagonal slices
and are denoted by
f(\reg j,\sing i):X(\reg j)\to Y(\sing i)
, where
i\in\mathbf{m}
and
j\in f_{\mathsf{r}}([i,i+1])
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg 2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing 2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg 3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing 3)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg 4)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 3)}

Figure 7:
A zigzag map
f:X\to Y
going up with the singular slices in red and regular slices in blue.

Given a category
\mathbf{C}
, we define its
zigzag category

\Zig(\mathbf{C})
to be the category whose objects are zigzags and whose morphisms are zigzag maps in
\mathbf{C}
.
This is functorial, i.e. we have a functor

\Zig:\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{Cat}

If we iterate
n
times, we obtain the
n
-fold zigzag category

\Zig^{n}(\mathbf{C})
.

Note that
\Zig(\mathbf{1})
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{\Delta}
, since a zigzag in the terminal category is uniquely determined by its length.
Since every category
\mathbf{C}
has a unique functor
\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{1}
, this naturally induces a functor

\pi:\Zig(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{\Delta}

sending a zigzag to its length and a zigzag map to its singular map.

Globularity

The original definition of zigzag maps
[
13
]
required all regular slices to be identities, a condition we drop here.
We call such zigzag maps
globular
, and we write
\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
for the wide subcategory of globular maps.

Definition 32
.

Let
X
be a zigzag of length
n
.
Its
restriction

X[a,b]
, for
a,b\in\mathbf{n+1}
with
a\leq b
, is defined to be the zigzag restricted to the cospans between the regular objects
X(\reg a)
and
X(\reg b)
.

Definition 33
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a zigzag map with
Y
of length
m
, and let
a,b\in\mathbf{m+1}
with
a<b
.
We define the
restriction

f[a,b]:X[c,d]\to Y[a,b]

for
[c,d]=f_{\mathsf{r}}([a,b])
, to be the zigzag map restricted to everything between
f(\reg a):X(\reg c)\to Y(\reg a)
and
f(\reg b):X(\reg d)\to Y(\reg b)
.

3.1
Diagrams

Let
\Sigma
be a set of labels equipped with a dimension function

\dim:\Sigma\to\mathbb{N}

This induces a partial order given by
f<g
iff
\dim f<\dim g
.

Definition 34
.

A
\Sigma
-typed
n
-diagram
is an object of
\Zig^{n}_{=}(\Sigma)
.

We think of
\Sigma
as encoding a signature and the objects of
\Zig^{n}_{=}(\Sigma)
as
n
-dimensional string diagrams in the free associative
n
-category generated by that signature.
Not all
n
-diagrams are valid, and the full theory of associative
n
-categories has a
typechecking
procedure for checking if an
n
-diagram is
well-typed
with respect to a signature.
For a detailed description, see
[
5
, Chapter 8]
and
[
10
, Section 7]
.

Example 35
.

Consider the 2-category generated by:

•

a 0-cell
x
,

•

a 1-cell
f:x\to x
, and

•

a 2-cell
m:f\circ f\to f
(i.e. a monoid).

The 2-cell
m
is represented by the following 2-diagram:
x
f
x
f
x
x
m
x
x
f
x

where
\Sigma=\{x,f,m\}
with
\dim x=0
,
\dim f=1
, and
\dim m=2
.

Example 36
.

Consider the 3-category generated by:

•

a 0-cell
x
, and

•

a pair of 2-cells
\alpha,\beta:\id x\to\id x
(i.e. two scalars).

The
Eckmann-Hilton move
is the 3-cell represented by the following 3-diagram, which can be interpreted as “braiding
\alpha
over
\beta
”:
x
x
\alpha
x
x
x
\beta
x
x
x
x
\alpha
x
\beta
x
x
x
x
\beta
x
x
x
\alpha
x
x

where
\Sigma=\{x,\alpha,\beta\}
with
\dim x=0
,
\dim\alpha=2
, and
\dim\beta=2
.

3.2
Explosion

Given a diagram in the zigzag category
\Zig(\mathbf{C})
, we can “explode” it to obtain a diagram in
\mathbf{C}
.
This can be understood as the left adjoint of the zigzag construction, a result which is inspired by the work of Heidemann
[
9
]
, however the presentation here is original.

Definition 37
.

Given a category
\mathbf{C}
with a functor
F:\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
, we define its
explosion

\Exp_{F}(\mathbf{C})
to be the category consisting of:

•

for every
x
in the fibre of
n
, objects

\displaystyle\sing^{x}_{i}

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle\reg^{x}_{j}

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{n+1}

•

for every
f:x\to y
in the fibre of
\alpha:\mathbf{n}\to\mathbf{m}
, morphisms

\displaystyle\sing^{x}_{i}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sing^{f}_{i}}\sing^{y}_{\alpha(i)}

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{n}

\displaystyle\reg^{x}_{\Reg\alpha(j)}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\reg^{f}_{j}}\reg^{y}_{j}

\displaystyle j

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{m+1}

\displaystyle\reg^{x}_{j}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\delta^{f}_{i,j}}\sing^{y}_{i}

\displaystyle i

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{m},\,j\in\Reg\alpha([i,i+1])

\mathbf{3}
\mathbf{4}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\mapsto
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 8:
A diagram
F:\mathbf{2}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
together with its explosion
\Exp_{F}(\mathbf{2})
.

Proposition 38
.

We have an adjunction

\Exp:\mathbf{Cat}/\mathbf{\Delta}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Cat}:\Zig

Proof.

For every
\mathbf{J}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
and
\mathbf{C}
, we define a bijection

F:\mathbf{J}\to\Zig(\mathbf{C})

\smallint F:\Exp(\mathbf{J})\to\mathbf{C}

Given a diagram
F
, we define the diagram
\smallint F
as follows:

\displaystyle\smallint F(\sing^{x}_{i})

\displaystyle\coloneqq F(x)(\sing i)

\displaystyle\smallint F(\reg^{x}_{j})

\displaystyle\coloneqq F(x)(\reg j)

\displaystyle\smallint F(\sing^{f}_{i})

\displaystyle\coloneqq F(f)(\sing i)

\displaystyle\smallint F(\reg^{f}_{j})

\displaystyle\coloneqq F(f)(\reg j)

\displaystyle\smallint F(\delta^{f}_{i,j})

\displaystyle\coloneqq F(f)(\reg j,\sing i)

It is easy to check this is a natural isomorphism, so
\Exp\dashv\Zig
.
∎

Explosion can be iterated to also explode diagrams in
\Zig^{n}(\mathbf{C})
:

F:\mathbf{J}\to\Zig^{n}(\mathbf{C})

\smallint^{k}F:\Exp^{k}(\mathbf{J})\to\Zig^{n-k}(\mathbf{C})

We call this the
k
-fold explosion
.
In particular, note that:

•

If
X
is an
n
-zigzag, seen as a functor
\mathbf{1}\to\Zig^{n}(\mathbf{C})
, its
n
-fold explosion
\Exp^{n}(\mathbf{1})\to\mathbf{C}
is
X
seen as a diagram in
\mathbf{C}
.

•

If
f
is an
n
-zigzag map, seen as a functor
\mathbf{2}\to\Zig^{n}(\mathbf{C})
, its
n
-fold explosion
\Exp^{n}(\mathbf{2})\to\mathbf{C}
is
f
seen as a diagram in
\mathbf{C}
.

3.3
Contraction

Recall the definition of contraction by Reutter and Vicary
[
13
]
.

Definition 39
.

Let
X
be a zigzag in
\mathbf{C}
.
We define its
contraction
to be the zigzag map arising from taking the colimit in
\mathbf{C}
, if it exists, of the underlying diagram
\smallint X:\Exp(\mathbf{1})\to\mathbf{C}
, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg n)}

If the colimit does not exist, then the contraction is not defined.

Reutter and Vicary have also given a procedure which computes connected colimits in
\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
.
This allows contraction to work on
n
-diagrams for every
n
.
There are three main steps to this procedure, which can be stated as the following technical lemmas.

Lemma 40
.

If
\mathbf{C}
is a disjoint union of categories, each having a terminal object, then
\pi:\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathbf{\Delta}
preserves connected colimits.

Proof.

See
[
13
, Proposition 39]
.
∎

Lemma 41
.

Consider a connected diagram
F:\mathbf{J}\to\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
and a cocone
\eta:F\Rightarrow\Delta_{X}
such that
\pi\circ\eta
is a colimit in
\mathbf{\Delta}
.
Then
\eta
is a colimit iff for every
i\in\lvert X\rvert
, the following cocone is a colimit:

\eta^{i}:F^{i}\Rightarrow\Delta_{X[i,i+1]}:\mathbf{J}\to\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})

with each
\eta^{i}_{x}:F^{i}_{x}\to X[i,i+1]
given by the restriction
\eta_{x}[i,i+1]
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
13
, Theorem 33]
.
∎

Lemma 42
.

Consider a connected diagram
F:\mathbf{J}\to\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
and a cocone
\eta:F\Rightarrow\Delta_{X}
such that
\pi\circ\eta
is a colimit in
\mathbf{\Delta}
and
\lvert X\rvert=1
.
Then
\eta
is a colimit iff the following cocone is a colimit in
\mathbf{C}
:

\smallint\eta:\smallint F\Rightarrow\Delta_{X(\sing 0)}:\Exp(\mathbf{J})\to\mathbf{C}

where the components are given by the singular and diagonal slices:

\displaystyle\smallint\eta(\sing^{x}_{i})

\displaystyle\coloneqq F_{x}(\sing i)\xrightarrow{\eta_{x}(\sing i)}X(\sing 0)

\displaystyle\smallint\eta(\reg^{x}_{j})

\displaystyle\coloneqq F_{x}(\reg j)\xrightarrow{\eta_{x}(\reg j,\sing 0)}X(\sing 0)

Proof.

This follows from
[
13
, Theorem 33]
.
∎

Therefore, the colimit of a connected diagram
F:\mathbf{J}\to\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
is obtained by taking a colimit in
\mathbf{\Delta}
, say
n
, and then an
\mathbf{n}
-indexed family of colimits in
\mathbf{C}
, namely the colimits of the explosions
\smallint F^{i}
.
If any of these colimits do not exist, neither does the colimit of
F
.

4
Anticontraction

We now define the dual of contraction, called
anticontraction
.
Just as contraction is based on colimits, this is based on anticolimits.

Definition 43
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define the following hypergraph:

V\coloneqq\mathbf{n+1}\qquad E\coloneqq\mathbf{n}\qquad i(e)=\{e,e+1\}

Let
\mathbf{J}_{n}
be the associated poset, which we can sketch as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

Definition 44
.

Let
X
be a zigzag map of length 1 in
\mathbf{C}
together with a sink in
\mathbf{C}
into the unique singular object of
X
:

\{A_{i}\to X(\sing 0)\}_{i\in\mathbf{n+1}}

An
anticontraction
of
X
is a zigzag map whose singular slices are
\{A_{i}\to X(\sing 0)\}
such that it is a contraction of its source, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}

In particular, an anticontraction consists of the following data:

•

a
\mathbf{J}_{n}
-anticolimit of the sink
\{A_{i}\to X(\sing 0)\}
,

•

a lift of
X(\reg 0)\to X(\sing 0)
along the morphism
A_{0}\to X(\sing 0)
,

•

a lift of
X(\reg 1)\to X(\sing 0)
along the morphism
A_{n}\to X(\sing 0)
.

4.1
Zigzag anticolimits

We give a procedure for computing anticolimits in
\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
, which is analogous to the procedure for colimits by Reutter and Vicary.

Theorem 45
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a disjoint union of categories, each having a terminal object.
Then for every sink of the following form

\kappa:X\Rightarrow\Delta_{Y}:\max\mathbf{J}\to\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})

for a connected poset
\mathbf{J}
, we have an embedding of categories

\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)\hookrightarrow\coprod\nolimits_{F\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\pi\circ\kappa)}\prod\nolimits_{i\in\lvert Y\rvert}\Acl_{\Exp_{F^{i}}(\mathbf{J})}(\smallint\kappa^{i})

i.e.
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimits of
\kappa
are uniquely determined by:

•

a
\mathbf{J}
-anticolimit
F
of
\pi\circ\kappa
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
, and

•

for every
i\in\lvert Y\rvert
, a
\Exp_{F^{i}}(\mathbf{J})
-anticolimit of
\smallint\kappa^{i}
in
\mathbf{C}
.

Note that
\smallint\kappa^{i}
is defined as in
Lemmas

41
and
42
, and
F^{i}:\mathbf{J}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
is the restriction of
F:\mathbf{J}\to\mathbf{\Delta}
to the preimages of
i
under
\pi\circ\kappa
.

Proof.

Let
A
be an anticolimit of
\kappa
in
\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
.
Then
F=\pi\circ A
is an anticolimit of
\pi\circ\kappa
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
by
Lemma

40
.
Moreover, for every
i\in\lvert Y\rvert
, the explosion of the restriction
\smallint A^{i}:\Exp_{F^{i}}(\mathbf{J})\to\mathbf{C}
is an anticolimit of
\smallint\kappa^{i}
in
\mathbf{C}
by
Lemmas

41
and
42
.
Hence, the map

A\mapsto(F,(\smallint A^{i})_{i\in\lvert Y\rvert})

is a well-defined map of the desired type.
The map is injective, as if
A\neq B
then either
\pi\circ A\neq\pi\circ B
or
\smallint A^{i}\neq\smallint B^{i}
for some
i
.
It is easy to see that this extends to a functor, and hence an embedding.
∎

While the embedding may fail to be epic, we can detect when something is in its image.
Suppose we are given the following:

F\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\pi\circ\kappa)\qquad G_{i}\in\Acl_{\Exp_{F^{i}}(\mathbf{J})}(\smallint\kappa^{i})

We can attempt to construct
A\in\Acl_{\mathbf{J}}(\kappa)
as follows:

1.

Unexplode each
G_{i}
to get a diagram
A_{i}:\mathbf{J}\to\Zig(\mathbf{C})
.

2.

Check that
A_{i}
is globular, otherwise fail.

3.

Then
\kappa^{i}
is a colimit of
A_{i}
by
Lemma

42
, so by globularity, every zigzag in
A_{i}
has the same first and last regular objects as
Y[i,i+1]
, i.e.
Y(\reg i)
and
Y(\reg i+1)
respectively.
Therefore, we can concatenate the
A_{i}
together to get a diagram

A:\mathbf{J}\to\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})

with
A^{i}=A_{i}
. Then by
Lemma

41
,
\kappa
is a colimit of
A
.

Therefore, anticolimits in
\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
can be computed in terms of anticolimits in
\mathbf{\Delta}
and
\mathbf{C}
.
Since we know how to do anticolimits in
\mathbf{\Delta}
, as shown in section 2, we can extract a recursive procedure for anticolimits in
\Zig^{n}_{=}(\mathbf{C})
in terms of anticolimits in
\mathbf{C}
, for all
n
.

4.2
Zigzag factorisation

We now show that if a category
\mathbf{C}
admits a factorisation structure for sinks, we can lift it to the globular zigzag category
\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
.
First, recall the definition of a factorisation structure for sinks, according to
[
4
, Section 3]
, as well as an useful technical result.

Definition 46
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a conglomerate of sinks in
\mathbf{C}
, and let
M
be a class of morphisms in
\mathbf{C}
, both closed under composition with isomorphisms.
Then
(\mathbf{E},M)
is a
factorisation structure
on
\mathbf{C}
if:

•

Every sink
\{f_{i}:A_{i}\to B\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathbf{C}
factors as
f_{i}=m\circ e_{i}
for a sink
\{e_{i}:A_{i}\to C\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathbf{E}
and a morphism
m:C\to B
in
M
.

•

(
\mathbf{E},M)
has the
orthogonal lifting property
, i.e. for every sink
\{e_{i}:A_{i}\to B\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathbf{E}
and morphism
m:C\to D
in
M
, if the outer square commutes for every
i
, there is a unique lift:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

Lemma 47
.

If
(\mathbf{E},M)
is a factorisation structure, then
M\subseteq\mathrm{Mono}
.

Proof.

See
[
4
, Proposition 3.6]
.
∎

Definition 48
.

If
\mathbf{E}
is a conglomerate of sinks and
M
is a class of morphisms in
\mathbf{C}
, then we define the following:

•

a sink
\{f_{i}:X_{i}\to Y\}_{i\in I}
in
\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
is
\mathbf{E}
-singular
if for every
k\in\lvert Y\rvert
, the sink of all singular slices into
Y(\sing k)
is in
\mathbf{E}
:

\{f_{i}(\sing j):X_{i}(\sing j)\to Y(\sing k)\mid i\in I,\,j\in(f_{i})_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1}(k)\}

•

a zigzag map is an
M
-relabelling
if it is globular, its singular map is the identity, and all its singular slices are in
M
.

We write
\Sing(\mathbf{E})
for the conglomerate of
\mathbf{E}
-singular sinks and
\Relab(M)
for the class of
M
-relabellings.
Note that these are both closed under composition with isomorphisms, if
\mathbf{E}
and
M
are.

Theorem 49
.

If
(\mathbf{E},M)
is a factorisation structure on
\mathbf{C}
, then we have a factorisation structure
(\Sing(\mathbf{E}),\Relab(M))
on
\Zig_{=}(\mathbf{C})
.

Proof.

Given a sink of globular zigzag maps
\{f_{i}:X_{i}\to Y\}_{i\in I}
, we want to define an
\mathbf{E}
-singular sink and an
M
-relabelling

\{e_{i}:X_{i}\to Z\}_{i\in I}\qquad m:Z\to Y

such that
f_{i}=m\circ e_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
In particular, we must have that

\displaystyle\lvert Z\rvert

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lvert Y\rvert

\displaystyle Z(\reg j)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Y(\reg j)

\displaystyle(e_{i})_{\mathsf{s}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(f_{i})_{\mathsf{s}}

\displaystyle m_{\mathsf{s}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\id

For every
k\in\lvert Y\rvert
, construct the sink of singular slices into
Y(\sing k)
:

\{f_{i}(\sing j):X_{i}(\sing j)\to Y(\sing k)\mid i\in I,\,j\in(f_{i})_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1}(k)\}\\

If this is empty, define
Z(\sing k)\coloneqq Y(\sing k)
and
m(\sing k)\coloneqq\id
.
Otherwise, we can factor this sink in
\mathbf{C}
as an
\mathbf{E}
-sink and an
M
-morphism:

\{e_{i}(\sing j):X_{i}(\sing j)\to Z(\sing k)\mid i\in I,\,j\in(f_{i})_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1}(k)\}\\

m(\sing k):Z(\sing k)\to Y(\sing k)

All that is left is to define the cospan maps

Z(\reg k)\to Z(\sing k)\qquad Z(\reg k+1)\to Z(\sing k)

such that they satisfy the following commutativity conditions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\reg k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\sing k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m(\sing k)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\reg k+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\sing k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\reg k+1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m(\sing k)}

(1)

If
(f_{i})_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1}(k)
is empty:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\reg k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\sing k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\reg k+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}(\reg(f_{i})_{\mathsf{r}}(k))}

(2)

If
(f_{i})_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1}(k)=[a,b]
is non-empty:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\reg k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\sing k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}(\reg a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}(\sing a)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\sing k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\reg k+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}(\sing b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}(\reg b+1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}(\sing b)}

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\sing k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}(\sing j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}(\reg j+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}(\sing j+1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}(\sing j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}(\sing j+1)}

(4)

If all preimages are empty, then
m(\sing k)=\id
, so we can define

\displaystyle Z(\reg k)\to Z(\sing k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Y(\reg k)\to Y(\sing k)

\displaystyle Z(\reg k+1)\to Z(\sing k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq Y(\reg k+1)\to Y(\sing k)

such that (1) and (2) hold.
Otherwise, if at least one of the preimages is non-empty, say
(f_{i})_{\mathsf{s}}^{-1}(k)=[a,b]
, then we can define

\displaystyle Z(\reg k)\to Z(\sing k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{i}(\reg a)\to X(\sing a)\xrightarrow{e_{i}(\sing a)}Z(\sing k)

\displaystyle Z(\reg k+1)\to Z(\sing k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{i}(\reg b+1)\to X(\sing b)\xrightarrow{e_{i}(\sing b)}Z(\sing k)

Now (1) always holds, and (2), (3), and (4) hold when post-composed with
m(\sing k)
, which is monic by
Lemma

47
, so they hold as well.

Finally, suppose that we have a family of commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

with
\{e_{i}\}\in\Sing(\mathbf{E})
and
m\in\Relab(M)
.
We want to define a lift:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

Since
m
is an
M
-relabelling, we necessarily have that
h_{\mathsf{s}}=g_{\mathsf{s}}
.
Now for every
k\in\lvert Y\rvert
and
j\in(g_{\mathsf{s}})^{-1}(k)
, the following square commutes, so by the orthogonal lifting property of
(\mathbf{E},M)
, it has a unique lift:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}(\sing j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\sing g_{\mathsf{s}}(k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(\sing k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(\sing g_{\mathsf{s}}(k))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}(\sing j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}(\sing j)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m(\sing g_{\mathsf{s}}(k))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(\sing k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(\sing k)}

Therefore,
h
is uniquely determined, concluding this proof.
∎

This factorisation enables us to propagate an anticontraction from a singular object of a zigzag to the whole zigzag.
This is called
recursive anticontraction
and is a generalisation of the recursive expansion procedure described in
[
13
, Section 5]
.

Recursive anticontraction

Suppose we are performing a recursive anticontraction on the singular object of a zigzag, so we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}

We lift this to a zigzag map according to the following scheme:

(a)

First, attempt to find lifts on both sides as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}

(b)

If one lift exists, say
X(\reg 0)\to A
but not the other, factorise the other morphism as
X(\reg 1)\to B\rightarrowtail X(\sing 0)
using the factorisation structure on zigzag maps, and then attempt to find the anticontraction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}

where
X(\reg 1)\to X(\sing 0)
factors as
X(\reg 1)\to B\rightarrowtail X(\sing 0)
.

(c)

As a fallback, if neither lift exists, or no anticontraction in (2) exists, then just introduce a “bubble” as follows, re-using the given map
A\to X(\sing 0)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\sing 0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(\reg 1)}

Step (b) here is novel, and introduces new capabilities to the proof assistant.
We provide the following example.
This is a zigzag map obtained by step (b): the first singular slice is a relabelling given by factorisation, and the second singular slice is another anticontraction.
Note that this is step 2 of the naturality move in
Figure

2
.
